---------- turn 19 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.4, F50.5\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is my eating has gotten really unpredictable. It sounds kind of small when I say it out loud, but it’s been bothering me. Some weeks I’ll barely touch dinner, like I’ll make food or sit down with it and then just kind of pick at it and feel like I can’t really eat. And then other nights it’s the total opposite—I’ll end up in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed, standing there eating crackers or sweets, not even really like I’m enjoying it, just doing it.\n\nIt’s been all over the place, and I don’t feel like I have a normal rhythm with food right now. That’s what pushed me to make the appointment.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think about your weight or body shape when you're deciding whether or not to eat?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way people usually mean, like counting calories or looking in the mirror and deciding I shouldn’t eat because of my weight. I mean, I’m aware of my body, sure, and I have the normal moments of not loving how I look, but when I’m skipping food or when I’m eating late at night, it doesn’t feel like it’s about trying to change my shape.\n\nIt’s more like I just don’t feel hungry, or food feels like too much effort, or then later I suddenly feel like I need something and I just grab whatever’s there. But I haven’t noticed myself thinking, “I can’t eat this because I’ll gain weight,” or anything like that. If that’s what you mean, then no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or ashamed after eating, especially during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel guilty, but it’s not always just about the eating itself. Like after the late-night stuff I’ll think, “Why did I do that? Why couldn’t I just go to bed like a normal person?” But the guilt kind of spreads out from there, if that makes sense.\n\nI can spend hours replaying really small things in my head. Like if I bought an expensive tube of pigment for painting or something, I’ll just go over it again and again, thinking I was selfish, that I wasted money, that I’ve somehow ruined the whole household budget even if it’s not actually that dramatic. Or if I snap at my spouse, even just a sharp tone, I’ll sit there afterward feeling like I’ve damaged everything, like I’m this terrible person and everyone would be better off if I could just… not be so much trouble.\n\nSo with food, yeah, there can be shame after I eat at night, but it’s more part of this bigger thing where I take something small and punish myself over it for hours.\n\nDoctor: During those late-night episodes of eating, do you feel like you're able to stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: Sometimes I can stop, but other times it feels like I sort of… drift into it. Like I’ll go into the kitchen thinking I’m just going to have a few crackers because I didn’t really eat dinner, and then I’m still standing there eating sweets or whatever’s easy to grab. It’s not planned, and it doesn’t feel very normal or controlled, I guess.\n\nIt’s not always a huge amount, but it’s the way it happens that bothers me. Some weeks I’m barely touching dinner, and then other nights after everyone’s gone to bed I’m in the kitchen eating like I’m trying to catch up or settle something. I don’t always feel like I made a clear decision to stop or start. It just kind of happens, and then afterward I’m left thinking, “Why did I do that?”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… some nights I can tell myself, “Okay, that’s enough,” and walk away, but on the nights I’m talking about, it doesn’t feel like I’m fully steering it.\n\nWhat finally made me feel like I needed to come in is that my eating has just gotten so all over the place. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, like I’ll make a plate or sit with everyone and then just push it around or take a few bites because nothing seems appealing. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’m standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets or whatever is easy, and it feels kind of automatic.\n\nIt’s not like I sit down and decide, “I’m going to eat a bunch now.” It’s more like I’m suddenly there, picking at things, and then I realize I’ve been doing it for a while. So I guess I’d say I don’t feel very in control during those episodes, even if it’s not always some huge amount of food. It’s the swing between barely eating and then eating late at night that scares me a little.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I haven’t been tracking it very carefully, honestly, so I don’t want to give you a number and be wrong. But I do feel like something has changed, mostly because my eating has been so inconsistent.\n\nLike, what finally pushed me to come in is that some weeks I’ll barely touch dinner. I’ll sit there with the plate in front of me and it just feels like too much, or nothing sounds good, and I end up picking at it. And then other nights it goes the opposite way, where everyone’s asleep and I’m standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets or whatever’s easy to grab.\n\nSo I don’t know if the weight change is “significant” in the way you mean, but my appetite and eating pattern feel really off compared to normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating would happen less often if you didn't worry about gaining weight from it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s mainly the weight worry that makes it happen, honestly. I mean, I do think about that afterward sometimes, like, “Great, now I’ve made everything worse,” but the thing that pushed me to come in is more that the whole eating pattern feels really all over the place.\n\nSome weeks I’m barely touching dinner at all, and then other nights, once everyone’s gone to bed, I’m standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets or whatever is right there. It feels less like, “I’m worried about gaining weight so now I’m doing this,” and more like something is just off with my appetite and the timing of it. Like I can’t get myself to eat normally earlier, and then later it sort of catches up with me in this messy way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat at night as a way to deal with stress or difficult emotions?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe sometimes, but I’m not totally sure that’s what starts it. It doesn’t feel like I’m sitting there thinking, “I’m upset, so I’m going to eat.” It’s more messy than that.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that my eating has just been really all over the place. Like some weeks I barely touch dinner — I’ll sit down with it and just kind of move food around, or take a couple bites and feel done. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed and the house is quiet, I end up in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets or whatever is easy to grab.\n\nI guess stress might be part of it, because late at night everything feels heavier and there’s nothing distracting me. But it also feels like my appetite is out of rhythm or something. Like I don’t eat normally earlier, and then later I’m just standing there eating without really planning to. So… maybe emotions play into it, but I wouldn’t say I fully understand why it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat at night even if you knew you wouldn't gain weight from it?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I think I probably would still do it sometimes, because it doesn’t feel completely tied to the weight part. I mean, I don’t love the idea of gaining weight from it, and sometimes afterward I do feel bad or frustrated with myself, but in the moment it’s not like I’m calculating that.\n\nIt’s more like… my eating has just been all over the place, and that’s what really made me feel like I needed to come in. Some weeks I’ll barely touch dinner, like I sit down and the food is there but I just can’t really make myself eat much. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’m in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets or whatever is easy, almost like my body is catching up at the wrong time.\n\nSo if you told me I definitely wouldn’t gain weight from it, I don’t think that would magically make the late-night eating stop. It might make me feel less guilty afterward, maybe, but the pattern itself still feels off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating patterns would change if you had a way to ensure you wouldn't gain weight, no matter what or how much you ate?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t think it would change the whole pattern, no. Maybe I’d feel less panicky or guilty afterward if there was some guarantee I wouldn’t gain weight, but the reason I’m sitting here isn’t just because I’m afraid of weight gain. It’s more that my eating feels really unpredictable, like I don’t have a normal rhythm with it anymore.\n\nSome weeks I barely touch dinner. I’ll make a plate, or someone will put food in front of me, and I’ll take a few bites and then just feel like I can’t keep going, or I’m not interested in it. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed and it’s quiet, I’ll end up in the kitchen eating crackers, sweets, whatever is easy to grab. It’s not even always like I’m enjoying it that much—it’s just happening.\n\nSo if there were some way to make sure I wouldn’t gain weight no matter what I ate, I think that might take away one layer of worry, but I don’t think it would fix the part where I’m not eating much earlier and then I’m standing there late at night eating random things. That’s the part that made me think something was off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating at night because you feel like you've lost control over your eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that might be part of it, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as completely losing control, like I’m in some kind of blackout or something. It’s more like the eating feels out of my normal control or rhythm, if that makes sense.\n\nWhat pushed me to come in is that it’s been really inconsistent. Some weeks I barely touch dinner — I’ll sit there with the plate in front of me and take a few bites, and then I just can’t get myself to eat more. And then on other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’ll be standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets, just whatever is easy to grab.\n\nIn those moments, it does feel like I’m not really making a clear decision. Like I didn’t plan it, I didn’t sit there thinking, “I’m going to go eat now.” I just end up there. So maybe that is a loss of control, but it feels less dramatic than that phrase sounds. It’s more that my eating feels all over the place, and I don’t feel like I’m steering it very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're usually eating at night because you're feeling anxious, sad, or upset at that moment?\n\nPatient: I mean… sometimes maybe, but I don’t think I can say it’s usually because I’m anxious or sad right then. It’s not always like I’m standing there thinking, “I’m upset, so I’m going to eat.” A lot of the time it feels more confusing than that.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that my eating has just been all over the place. Like, some weeks I barely touch dinner at all — I’ll sit there and take a few bites and then I’m just done, even if I know I probably should eat more. And then other nights, when everyone’s gone to bed and the house is quiet, I’ll end up in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets or whatever is easy to grab.\n\nSo there might be feelings mixed into it sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I can clearly connect it to being anxious, sad, or upset every time. It feels more like my appetite and timing are just really off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is what primarily drives you to eat at night?\n\nPatient: Not primarily, no. I mean, I don’t want to gain weight, and I’d be lying if I said that thought never comes up, especially after I’ve eaten more than I meant to. But I don’t think that’s the main thing driving the night eating.\n\nWhat finally made me feel like I should come in is that my eating has just gotten really… uneven. Some weeks I barely touch dinner. I’ll sit down with food and take a few bites, and then it’s like I just can’t keep eating or I don’t want it anymore. And then other nights, later on, when everyone’s gone to bed, I’ll find myself standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets or whatever is just sitting there.\n\nSo it doesn’t feel like, “I’m afraid of gaining weight, so I’m eating at night.” If anything, the fear shows up after, or around it, but it doesn’t explain why my appetite is so off or why I’m barely eating earlier and then eating random stuff late at night. That’s the part that feels confusing to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat at night because you're worried you didn't eat enough earlier in the day?\n\nPatient: Maybe partly, but I don’t think that’s exactly what’s going through my mind at the time. It’s not usually like I’m thinking, “Oh, I didn’t eat enough today, I better make up for it.” It feels less organized than that.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is just that my eating has been all over the place. Some weeks I barely touch dinner — like I’ll sit down, take a few bites, and then I just can’t seem to keep going. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed and the house is quiet, I’ll find myself standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets, just whatever is easy.\n\nSo I guess it could be connected to not eating enough earlier, but I’m not consciously worried about it in the moment. It’s more like my eating pattern is really uneven, and I don’t feel like I understand why.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat at night because you're trying to make up for not eating enough during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s that deliberate, exactly. Like, I’m not usually standing there at night thinking, “I didn’t eat enough today, so now I need to make up for it.” It’s more like the whole pattern has gotten really off, and that’s what finally made me feel like I should come in.\n\nSome weeks I barely touch dinner at all. I’ll sit down with food in front of me and only take a few bites, and then I’m just done — not in some planned way, just like I can’t get myself to keep eating. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed and the house is quiet, I end up in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets or whatever is easy to grab.\n\nSo maybe my body is trying to make up for it in some way, I don’t know. But in my head, it doesn’t feel like a clear plan. It just feels like my appetite and eating times are all over the place, and I don’t really understand why.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry when you eat at night?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, but not always. That’s part of what’s been bothering me, honestly, because it doesn’t feel like a normal pattern where I can say, “Yes, I’m hungry, so I eat.” Some nights I do feel that kind of empty feeling, especially if I barely ate dinner, but other nights it’s more like I just end up there in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed.\n\nI’ll be standing there eating crackers or something sweet, whatever is easy, and I don’t always know if it’s hunger exactly. It’s more that my eating has gotten really uneven. Some weeks I barely touch dinner — I’ll sit there and only have a few bites — and then later, or on other nights, I’m eating things I didn’t really plan to eat. That’s really what pushed me to come in, because I can’t tell if it’s hunger, habit, or just my appetite being completely off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your night eating is because you're worried you won't have enough food the next day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s that. I’m not usually worried that there won’t be food tomorrow or anything like that. It’s not like I’m thinking ahead in that way.\n\nIt’s more just… my eating has gotten really strange and uneven. That’s actually what finally made me feel like I needed to come in. Some weeks I barely touch dinner — I’ll sit there with the plate in front of me and take a few bites, and then I just can’t seem to keep eating. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed and the house is quiet, I’ll end up in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets, just whatever is easy to grab.\n\nSo it doesn’t feel like fear about tomorrow’s food. It feels more like I don’t have a normal rhythm with eating right now, and I don’t really understand why it keeps swinging like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your night eating happens because you're worried you won't eat enough the next day?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don’t think I’m worried that I won’t eat enough the next day. It doesn’t feel like I’m planning ahead or thinking, “I better eat now because tomorrow I won’t.” It’s more confusing than that.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that my eating has just been all over the place. Some weeks I barely touch dinner — like I’ll sit there with food in front of me and only take a few bites, and then I just sort of stop. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’m standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets, whatever is easy to grab. It feels less like worry about tomorrow and more like I don’t have any normal rhythm with eating right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you start during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m completely unable to stop, like I’m out of control every single time. It’s not always like that. But there are nights where once I’m in the kitchen and I start eating, it kind of keeps going longer than I meant it to. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to have a few crackers, or just one sweet thing, and then I’m still standing there picking at more.\n\nThe bigger thing, I guess, is that my eating has just been all over the place. That’s really what pushed me to come in. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, like I’ll sit with the plate there and only manage a few bites. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’m in the kitchen eating crackers and sweets because they’re easy and right there.\n\nSo… sometimes I can stop, but it doesn’t feel normal or steady. I’ll make a plate, or someone will put food in front of me, and I’ll take a few bites and then just feel like I can’t keep going, or I’m not interested in it. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed and it’s quiet, I’ll end up in the kitchen eating crackers, sweets, whatever is easy to grab. It’s not even always like I’m enjoying it that much—it’s just happening.\n\nSo if there were some way to make sure I wouldn’t gain weight no matter what I ate, I think that might take away one layer of worry, but I don’t think it would fix the part where I’m not eating much earlier and then I’m standing there late at night eating random things. That’s the part that made me think something was off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating at night because you feel like you've lost control over your eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that might be part of it, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as completely losing control, like I’m in some kind of blackout or something. It’s more like the eating feels out of my normal control or rhythm, if that makes sense.\n\nWhat pushed me to come in is that it’s been really inconsistent. Some weeks I barely touch dinner — I’ll sit there with the plate in front of me and take a few bites, and then I just can’t get myself to eat more. And then on other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’ll be standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets, just whatever is easy to grab.\n\nIn those moments, it does feel like I’m not really making a clear decision. Like I didn’t plan it, I didn’t sit there thinking, “I’m going to go eat now.” I just end up there. So maybe that is a loss of control, but it feels less dramatic than that phrase sounds. It’s more that my eating feels all over the place, and I don’t feel like I’m steering it very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're usually eating at night because you're feeling anxious, sad, or upset at that moment?\n\nPatient: I mean… sometimes maybe, but I don’t think I can say it’s usually because I’m anxious or sad right then. It’s not always like I’m standing there thinking, “I’m upset, so I’m going to eat.” A lot of the time it feels more confusing than that.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that my eating has just been all over the place. Like, some weeks I barely touch dinner at all — I’ll sit there and take a few bites and then I’m just done, even if I know I probably should eat more. And then other nights, when everyone’s gone to bed and the house is quiet, I’ll end up in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets or whatever is easy to grab.\n\nSo there might be feelings mixed into it sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I can clearly connect it to being anxious, sad, or upset every time. It feels more like my appetite and timing are just really off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is what primarily drives you to eat at night?\n\nPatient: Not primarily, no. I mean, I don’t want to gain weight, and I’d be lying if I said that thought never comes up, especially after I’ve eaten more than I meant to. But I don’t think that’s the main thing driving the night eating.\n\nWhat finally made me feel like I should come in is that my eating has just gotten really… uneven. Some weeks I barely touch dinner. I’ll sit down with food and take a few bites, and then it’s like I just can’t keep eating or I don’t want it anymore. And then other nights, later on, when everyone’s gone to bed, I’ll find myself standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets or whatever is just sitting there.\n\nSo it doesn’t feel like, “I’m afraid of gaining weight, so I’m eating at night.” If anything, the fear shows up after, or around it, but it doesn’t explain why my appetite is so off or why I’m barely eating earlier and then eating random stuff late at night. That’s the part that feels confusing to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat at night because you're worried you didn't eat enough earlier in the day?\n\nPatient: Maybe partly, but I don’t think that’s exactly what’s going through my mind at the time. It’s not usually like I’m thinking, “Oh, I didn’t eat enough today, I better make up for it.” It feels less organized than that.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is just that my eating has been all over the place. Some weeks I barely touch dinner — like I’ll sit down, take a few bites, and then I just can’t seem to keep going. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed and the house is quiet, I’ll find myself standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets, just whatever is easy.\n\nSo I guess it could be connected to not eating enough earlier, but I’m not consciously worried about it in the moment. It’s more like my eating pattern is really uneven, and I don’t feel like I understand why.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat at night because you're trying to make up for not eating enough during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s that deliberate, exactly. Like, I’m not usually standing there at night thinking, “I didn’t eat enough today, so now I need to make up for it.” It’s more like the whole pattern has gotten really off, and that’s what finally made me feel like I should come in.\n\nSome weeks I barely touch dinner at all. I’ll sit down with food in front of me and only take a few bites, and then I’m just done — not in some planned way, just like I can’t get myself to keep eating. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed and the house is quiet, I end up in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets or whatever is easy to grab.\n\nSo maybe my body is trying to make up for it in some way, I don’t know. But in my head, it doesn’t feel like a clear plan. It just feels like my appetite and eating times are all over the place, and I don’t really understand why.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry when you eat at night?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, but not always. That’s part of what’s been bothering me, honestly, because it doesn’t feel like a normal pattern where I can say, “Yes, I’m hungry, so I eat.” Some nights I do feel that kind of empty feeling, especially if I barely ate dinner, but other nights it’s more like I just end up there in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed.\n\nI’ll be standing there eating crackers or something sweet, whatever is easy, and I don’t always know if it’s hunger exactly. It’s more that my eating has gotten really uneven. Some weeks I barely touch dinner — I’ll sit there and only have a few bites — and then later, or on other nights, I’m eating things I didn’t really plan to eat. That’s really what pushed me to come in, because I can’t tell if it’s hunger, habit, or just my appetite being completely off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your night eating is because you're worried you won't have enough food the next day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s that. I’m not usually worried that there won’t be food tomorrow or anything like that. It’s not like I’m thinking ahead in that way.\n\nIt’s more just… my eating has gotten really strange and uneven. That’s actually what finally made me feel like I needed to come in. Some weeks I barely touch dinner — I’ll sit there with the plate in front of me and take a few bites, and then I just can’t seem to keep eating. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed and the house is quiet, I’ll end up in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets, just whatever is easy to grab.\n\nSo it doesn’t feel like fear about tomorrow’s food. It feels more like I don’t have a normal rhythm with eating right now, and I don’t really understand why it keeps swinging like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your night eating happens because you're worried you won't eat enough the next day?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don’t think I’m worried that I won’t eat enough the next day. It doesn’t feel like I’m planning ahead or thinking, “I better eat now because tomorrow I won’t.” It’s more confusing than that.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that my eating has just been all over the place. Some weeks I barely touch dinner — like I’ll sit there with food in front of me and only take a few bites, and then I just sort of stop. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… I mean, weight is in my mind a little, because it’s hard not to think about that when your eating feels off. But I don’t think the night eating is only about gaining weight or not gaining weight.

It’s more that my eating has just been really uneven, and that’s what finally made me feel like I needed to come in. Some weeks I barely eat dinner — I’ll sit there with the plate in front of me and just pick at it, maybe a few bites, and then I’m done even if I know I should probably eat more. And then other nights, after everyone has gone to bed and it’s quiet, I end up standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets, whatever is easy to grab.

So if someone told me, “You won’t gain any weight from this,” I don’t know that it would stop. Maybe I’d feel less guilty about it, but the pattern would probably still be there, because it doesn’t feel like it’s just about weight. It feels like I can’t get into a normal rhythm with food.

And then other nights it’s the total opposite—I’ll end up in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed, standing there eating crackers or sweets, not even really like I’m enjoying it, just doing it.\n\nIt’s been all over the place, and I don’t feel like I have a normal rhythm with food right now. That’s what pushed me to make the appointment.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think about your weight or body shape when you're deciding whether or not to eat?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way people usually mean, like counting calories or looking in the mirror and deciding I shouldn’t eat because of my weight. I mean, I’m aware of my body, sure, and I have the normal moments of not loving how I look, but when I’m skipping food or when I’m eating late at night, it doesn’t feel like it’s about trying to change my shape.\n\nIt’s more like I just don’t feel hungry, or food feels like too much effort, or then later I suddenly feel like I need something and I just grab whatever’s there. But I haven’t noticed myself thinking, “I can’t eat this because I’ll gain weight,” or anything like that. If that’s what you mean, then no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or ashamed after eating, especially during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel guilty, but it’s not always just about the eating itself. Like after the late-night stuff I’ll think, “Why did I do that? Why couldn’t I just go to bed like a normal person?” But the guilt kind of spreads out from there, if that makes sense.\n\nI can spend hours replaying really small things in my head. Like if I bought an expensive tube of pigment for painting or something, I’ll just go over it again and again, thinking I was selfish, that I wasted money, that I’ve somehow ruined the whole household budget even if it’s not actually that dramatic. Or if I snap at my spouse, even just a sharp tone, I’ll sit there afterward feeling like I’ve damaged everything, like I’m this terrible person and everyone would be better off if I could just… not be so much trouble.\n\nSo with food, yeah, there can be shame after I eat at night, but it’s more part of this bigger thing where I take something small and punish myself over it for hours.\n\nDoctor: During those late-night episodes of eating, do you feel like you're able to stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: Sometimes I can stop, but other times it feels like I sort of… drift into it. Like I’ll go into the kitchen thinking I’m just going to have a few crackers because I didn’t really eat dinner, and then I’m still standing there eating sweets or whatever’s easy to grab. It’s not planned, and it doesn’t feel very normal or controlled, I guess.\n\nIt’s not always a huge amount, but it’s the way it happens that bothers me. Some weeks I’m barely touching dinner, and then other nights after everyone’s gone to bed I’m in the kitchen eating like I’m trying to catch up or settle something. I don’t always feel like I made a clear decision to stop or start. It just kind of happens, and then afterward I’m left thinking, “Why did I do that?”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… some nights I can tell myself, “Okay, that’s enough,” and walk away, but on the nights I’m talking about, it doesn’t feel like I’m fully steering it.\n\nWhat finally made me feel like I needed to come in is that my eating has just gotten so all over the place. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, like I’ll make a plate or sit with everyone and then just push it around or take a few bites because nothing seems appealing. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’m standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets or whatever is easy, and it feels kind of automatic.\n\nIt’s not like I sit down and decide, “I’m going to eat a bunch now.” It’s more like I’m suddenly there, picking at things, and then I realize I’ve been doing it for a while. So I guess I’d say I don’t feel very in control during those episodes, even if it’s not always some huge amount of food. It’s the swing between barely eating and then eating late at night that scares me a little.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I haven’t been tracking it very carefully, honestly, so I don’t want to give you a number and be wrong. But I do feel like something has changed, mostly because my eating has been so inconsistent.\n\nLike, what finally pushed me to come in is that some weeks I’ll barely touch dinner. I’ll sit there with the plate in front of me and it just feels like too much, or nothing sounds good, and I end up picking at it. And then other nights it goes the opposite way, where everyone’s asleep and I’m standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets or whatever’s easy to grab.\n\nSo I don’t know if the weight change is “significant” in the way you mean, but my appetite and eating pattern feel really off compared to normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating would happen less often if you didn't worry about gaining weight from it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s mainly the weight worry that makes it happen, honestly. I mean, I do think about that afterward sometimes, like, “Great, now I’ve made everything worse,” but the thing that pushed me to come in is more that the whole eating pattern feels really all over the place.\n\nSome weeks I’m barely touching dinner at all, and then other nights, once everyone’s gone to bed, I’m standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets or whatever is right there. It feels less like, “I’m worried about gaining weight so now I’m doing this,” and more like something is just off with my appetite and the timing of it. Like I can’t get myself to eat normally earlier, and then later it sort of catches up with me in this messy way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat at night as a way to deal with stress or difficult emotions?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe sometimes, but I’m not totally sure that’s what starts it. It doesn’t feel like I’m sitting there thinking, “I’m upset, so I’m going to eat.” It’s more messy than that.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that my eating has just been really all over the place. Like some weeks I barely touch dinner — I’ll sit down with it and just kind of move food around, or take a couple bites and feel done. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed and the house is quiet, I end up in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets or whatever is easy to grab.\n\nI guess stress might be part of it, because late at night everything feels heavier and there’s nothing distracting me. But it also feels like my appetite is out of rhythm or something. Like I don’t eat normally earlier, and then later I’m just standing there eating without really planning to. So… maybe emotions play into it, but I wouldn’t say I fully understand why it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat at night even if you knew you wouldn't gain weight from it?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I think I probably would still do it sometimes, because it doesn’t feel completely tied to the weight part. I mean, I don’t love the idea of gaining weight from it, and sometimes afterward I do feel bad or frustrated with myself, but in the moment it’s not like I’m calculating that.\n\nIt’s more like… my eating has just been all over the place, and that’s what really made me feel like I needed to come in. Some weeks I’ll barely touch dinner, like I sit down and the food is there but I just can’t really make myself eat much. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’m in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets or whatever is easy, almost like my body is catching up at the wrong time.\n\nSo if you told me I definitely wouldn’t gain weight from it, I don’t think that would magically make the late-night eating stop. It might make me feel less guilty afterward, maybe, but the pattern itself still feels off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating patterns would change if you had a way to ensure you wouldn't gain weight, no matter what or how much you ate?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t think it would change the whole pattern, no. Maybe I’d feel less panicky or guilty afterward if there was some guarantee I wouldn’t gain weight, but the reason I’m sitting here isn’t just because I’m afraid of weight gain. It’s more that my eating feels really unpredictable, like I don’t have a normal rhythm with it anymore.\n\nSome weeks I barely touch dinner. I’ll make a plate, or someone will put food in front of me, and I’ll take a few bites and then just feel like I can’t keep going, or I’m not interested in it. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed and it’s quiet, I’ll end up in the kitchen eating crackers, sweets, whatever is easy to grab. It’s not even always like I’m enjoying it that much—it’s just happening.\n\nSo if there were some way to make sure I wouldn’t gain weight no matter what I ate, I think that might take away one layer of worry, but I don’t think it would fix the part where I’m not eating much earlier and then I’m standing there late at night eating random things. That’s the part that made me think something was off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating at night because you feel like you've lost control over your eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that might be part of it, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as completely losing control, like I’m in some kind of blackout or something. It’s more like the eating feels out of my normal control or rhythm, if that makes sense.\n\nWhat pushed me to come in is that it’s been really inconsistent. Some weeks I barely touch dinner — I’ll sit there with the plate in front of me and take a few bites, and then I just can’t get myself to eat more. And then on other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’ll be standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets, just whatever is easy to grab.\n\nIn those moments, it does feel like I’m not really making a clear decision. Like I didn’t plan it, I didn’t sit there thinking, “I’m going to go eat now.” I just end up there. So maybe that is a loss of control, but it feels less dramatic than that phrase sounds. It’s more that my eating feels all over the place, and I don’t feel like I’m steering it very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're usually eating at night because you're feeling anxious, sad, or upset at that moment?\n\nPatient: I mean… sometimes maybe, but I don’t think I can say it’s usually because I’m anxious or sad right then. It’s not always like I’m standing there thinking, “I’m upset, so I’m going to eat.” A lot of the time it feels more confusing than that.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that my eating has just been all over the place. Like, some weeks I barely touch dinner at all — I’ll sit there and take a few bites and then I’m just done, even if I know I probably should eat more. And then other nights, when everyone’s gone to bed and the house is quiet, I’ll end up in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets or whatever is easy to grab.\n\nSo there might be feelings mixed into it sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I can clearly connect it to being anxious, sad, or upset every time. It feels more like my appetite and timing are just really off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is what primarily drives you to eat at night?\n\nPatient: Not primarily, no. I mean, I don’t want to gain weight, and I’d be lying if I said that thought never comes up, especially after I’ve eaten more than I meant to. But I don’t think that’s the main thing driving the night eating.\n\nWhat finally made me feel like I should come in is that my eating has just gotten really… uneven. Some weeks I barely touch dinner. I’ll sit down with food and take a few bites, and then it’s like I just can’t keep eating or I don’t want it anymore. And then other nights, later on, when everyone’s gone to bed, I’ll find myself standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets or whatever is just sitting there.\n\nSo it doesn’t feel like, “I’m afraid of gaining weight, so I’m eating at night.” If anything, the fear shows up after, or around it, but it doesn’t explain why my appetite is so off or why I’m barely eating earlier and then eating random stuff late at night. That’s the part that feels confusing to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat at night because you're worried you didn't eat enough earlier in the day?\n\nPatient: Maybe partly, but I don’t think that’s exactly what’s going through my mind at the time. It’s not usually like I’m thinking, “Oh, I didn’t eat enough today, I better make up for it.” It feels less organized than that.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is just that my eating has been all over the place. Some weeks I barely touch dinner — like I’ll sit down, take a few bites, and then I just can’t seem to keep going. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed and the house is quiet, I’ll find myself standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets, just whatever is easy.\n\nSo I guess it could be connected to not eating enough earlier, but I’m not consciously worried about it in the moment. It’s more like my eating pattern is really uneven, and I don’t feel like I understand why.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat at night because you're trying to make up for not eating enough during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s that deliberate, exactly. Like, I’m not usually standing there at night thinking, “I didn’t eat enough today, so now I need to make up for it.” It’s more like the whole pattern has gotten really off, and that’s what finally made me feel like I should come in.\n\nSome weeks I barely touch dinner at all. I’ll sit down with food in front of me and only take a few bites, and then I’m just done — not in some planned way, just like I can’t get myself to keep eating. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed and the house is quiet, I end up in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets or whatever is easy to grab.\n\nSo maybe my body is trying to make up for it in some way, I don’t know. But in my head, it doesn’t feel like a clear plan. It just feels like my appetite and eating times are all over the place, and I don’t really understand why.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry when you eat at night?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, but not always. That’s part of what’s been bothering me, honestly, because it doesn’t feel like a normal pattern where I can say, “Yes, I’m hungry, so I eat.” Some nights I do feel that kind of empty feeling, especially if I barely ate dinner, but other nights it’s more like I just end up there in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed.\n\nI’ll be standing there eating crackers or something sweet, whatever is easy, and I don’t always know if it’s hunger exactly. It’s more that my eating has gotten really uneven. Some weeks I barely touch dinner — I’ll sit there and only have a few bites — and then later, or on other nights, I’m eating things I didn’t really plan to eat. That’s really what pushed me to come in, because I can’t tell if it’s hunger, habit, or just my appetite being completely off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your night eating is because you're worried you won't have enough food the next day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s that. I’m not usually worried that there won’t be food tomorrow or anything like that. It’s not like I’m thinking ahead in that way.\n\nIt’s more just… my eating has gotten really strange and uneven. That’s actually what finally made me feel like I needed to come in. Some weeks I barely touch dinner — I’ll sit there with the plate in front of me and take a few bites, and then I just can’t seem to keep eating. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed and the house is quiet, I’ll end up in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets, just whatever is easy to grab.\n\nSo it doesn’t feel like fear about tomorrow’s food. It feels more like I don’t have a normal rhythm with eating right now, and I don’t really understand why it keeps swinging like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your night eating happens because you're worried you won't eat enough the next day?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don’t think I’m worried that I won’t eat enough the next day. It doesn’t feel like I’m planning ahead or thinking, “I better eat now because tomorrow I won’t.” It’s more confusing than that.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that my eating has just been all over the place. Some weeks I barely touch dinner — like I’ll sit there with food in front of me and only take a few bites, and then I just sort of stop. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’m standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets, whatever is easy to grab. It feels less like worry about tomorrow and more like I don’t have any normal rhythm with eating right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you start during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m completely unable to stop, like I’m out of control every single time. It’s not always like that. But there are nights where once I’m in the kitchen and I start eating, it kind of keeps going longer than I meant it to. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to have a few crackers, or just one sweet thing, and then I’m still standing there picking at more.\n\nThe bigger thing, I guess, is that my eating has just been all over the place. That’s really what pushed me to come in. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, like I’ll sit with the plate there and only manage a few bites. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’m in the kitchen eating crackers and sweets because they’re easy and right there.\n\nSo… sometimes I can stop, but it doesn’t feel normal or steady. It feels like I don’t have a good handle on the pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your night eating would stop if you knew you wouldn't gain any weight from it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, weight is in my mind a little, because it’s hard not to think about that when your eating feels off. But I don’t think the night eating is only about gaining weight or not gaining weight.\n\nIt’s more that my eating has just been really uneven, and that’s what finally made me feel like I needed to come in. Some weeks I barely eat dinner — I’ll sit there with the plate in front of me and just pick at it, maybe a few bites, and then I’m done even if I know I should probably eat more. And then other nights, after everyone has gone to bed and it’s quiet, I end up standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets, whatever is easy to grab.\n\nSo if someone told me, “You won’t gain any weight from this,” I don’t know that it would stop. Maybe I’d feel less guilty about it, but the pattern would probably still be there, because it doesn’t feel like it’s just about weight. It feels like I can’t get into a normal rhythm with food.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I’ll make a plate, or someone will put food in front of me, and I’ll take a few bites and then just feel like I can’t keep going, or I’m not interested in it. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed and it’s quiet, I’ll end up in the kitchen eating crackers, sweets, whatever is easy to grab. It’s not even always like I’m enjoying it that much—it’s just happening.\n\nSo if there were some way to make sure I wouldn’t gain weight no matter what I ate, I think that might take away one layer of worry, but I don’t think it would fix the part where I’m not eating much earlier and then I’m standing there late at night eating random things. That’s the part that made me think something was off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating at night because you feel like you've lost control over your eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that might be part of it, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as completely losing control, like I’m in some kind of blackout or something. It’s more like the eating feels out of my normal control or rhythm, if that makes sense.\n\nWhat pushed me to come in is that it’s been really inconsistent. Some weeks I barely touch dinner — I’ll sit there with the plate in front of me and take a few bites, and then I just can’t get myself to eat more. And then on other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’ll be standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets, just whatever is easy to grab.\n\nIn those moments, it does feel like I’m not really making a clear decision. Like I didn’t plan it, I didn’t sit there thinking, “I’m going to go eat now.” I just end up there. So maybe that is a loss of control, but it feels less dramatic than that phrase sounds. It’s more that my eating feels all over the place, and I don’t feel like I’m steering it very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're usually eating at night because you're feeling anxious, sad, or upset at that moment?\n\nPatient: I mean… sometimes maybe, but I don’t think I can say it’s usually because I’m anxious or sad right then. It’s not always like I’m standing there thinking, “I’m upset, so I’m going to eat.” A lot of the time it feels more confusing than that.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that my eating has just been all over the place. Like, some weeks I barely touch dinner at all — I’ll sit there and take a few bites and then I’m just done, even if I know I probably should eat more. And then other nights, when everyone’s gone to bed and the house is quiet, I’ll end up in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets or whatever is easy to grab.\n\nSo there might be feelings mixed into it sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I can clearly connect it to being anxious, sad, or upset every time. It feels more like my appetite and timing are just really off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is what primarily drives you to eat at night?\n\nPatient: Not primarily, no. I mean, I don’t want to gain weight, and I’d be lying if I said that thought never comes up, especially after I’ve eaten more than I meant to. But I don’t think that’s the main thing driving the night eating.\n\nWhat finally made me feel like I should come in is that my eating has just gotten really… uneven. Some weeks I barely touch dinner. I’ll sit down with food and take a few bites, and then it’s like I just can’t keep eating or I don’t want it anymore. And then other nights, later on, when everyone’s gone to bed, I’ll find myself standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets or whatever is just sitting there.\n\nSo it doesn’t feel like, “I’m afraid of gaining weight, so I’m eating at night.” If anything, the fear shows up after, or around it, but it doesn’t explain why my appetite is so off or why I’m barely eating earlier and then eating random stuff late at night. That’s the part that feels confusing to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat at night because you're worried you didn't eat enough earlier in the day?\n\nPatient: Maybe partly, but I don’t think that’s exactly what’s going through my mind at the time. It’s not usually like I’m thinking, “Oh, I didn’t eat enough today, I better make up for it.” It feels less organized than that.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is just that my eating has been all over the place. Some weeks I barely touch dinner — like I’ll sit down, take a few bites, and then I just can’t seem to keep going. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed and the house is quiet, I’ll find myself standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets, just whatever is easy.\n\nSo I guess it could be connected to not eating enough earlier, but I’m not consciously worried about it in the moment. It’s more like my eating pattern is really uneven, and I don’t feel like I understand why.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat at night because you're trying to make up for not eating enough during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s that deliberate, exactly. Like, I’m not usually standing there at night thinking, “I didn’t eat enough today, so now I need to make up for it.” It’s more like the whole pattern has gotten really off, and that’s what finally made me feel like I should come in.\n\nSome weeks I barely touch dinner at all. I’ll sit down with food in front of me and only take a few bites, and then I’m just done — not in some planned way, just like I can’t get myself to keep eating. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed and the house is quiet, I end up in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets or whatever is easy to grab.\n\nSo maybe my body is trying to make up for it in some way, I don’t know. But in my head, it doesn’t feel like a clear plan. It just feels like my appetite and eating times are all over the place, and I don’t really understand why.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry when you eat at night?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, but not always. That’s part of what’s been bothering me, honestly, because it doesn’t feel like a normal pattern where I can say, “Yes, I’m hungry, so I eat.” Some nights I do feel that kind of empty feeling, especially if I barely ate dinner, but other nights it’s more like I just end up there in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed.\n\nI’ll be standing there eating crackers or something sweet, whatever is easy, and I don’t always know if it’s hunger exactly. It’s more that my eating has gotten really uneven. Some weeks I barely touch dinner — I’ll sit there and only have a few bites — and then later, or on other nights, I’m eating things I didn’t really plan to eat. That’s really what pushed me to come in, because I can’t tell if it’s hunger, habit, or just my appetite being completely off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your night eating is because you're worried you won't have enough food the next day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s that. I’m not usually worried that there won’t be food tomorrow or anything like that. It’s not like I’m thinking ahead in that way.\n\nIt’s more just… my eating has gotten really strange and uneven. That’s actually what finally made me feel like I needed to come in. Some weeks I barely touch dinner — I’ll sit there with the plate in front of me and take a few bites, and then I just can’t seem to keep eating. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed and the house is quiet, I’ll end up in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets, just whatever is easy to grab.\n\nSo it doesn’t feel like fear about tomorrow’s food. It feels more like I don’t have a normal rhythm with eating right now, and I don’t really understand why it keeps swinging like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your night eating happens because you're worried you won't eat enough the next day?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don’t think I’m worried that I won’t eat enough the next day. It doesn’t feel like I’m planning ahead or thinking, “I better eat now because tomorrow I won’t.” It’s more confusing than that.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that my eating has just been all over the place. Some weeks I barely touch dinner — like I’ll sit there with food in front of me and only take a few bites, and then I just sort of stop. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’m standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets, whatever is easy to grab. It feels less like worry about tomorrow and more like I don’t have any normal rhythm with eating right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you start during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m completely unable to stop, like I’m out of control every single time. It’s not always like that. But there are nights where once I’m in the kitchen and I start eating, it kind of keeps going longer than I meant it to. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to have a few crackers, or just one sweet thing, and then I’m still standing there picking at more.\n\nThe bigger thing, I guess, is that my eating has just been all over the place. That’s really what pushed me to come in. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, like I’ll sit with the plate there and only manage a few bites. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’m in the kitchen eating crackers and sweets because they’re easy and right there.\n\nSo… sometimes I can stop, but it doesn’t feel normal or steady. It feels like I don’t have a good handle on the pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your night eating would stop if you knew you wouldn't gain any weight from it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, weight is in my mind a little, because it’s hard not to think about that when your eating feels off. But I don’t think the night eating is only about gaining weight or not gaining weight.\n\nIt’s more that my eating has just been really uneven, and that’s what finally made me feel like I needed to come in. Some weeks I barely eat dinner — I’ll sit there with the plate in front of me and just pick at it, maybe a few bites, and then I’m done even if I know I should probably eat more. And then other nights, after everyone has gone to bed and it’s quiet, I end up standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets, whatever is easy to grab.\n\nSo if someone told me, “You won’t gain any weight from this,” I don’t know that it would stop. Maybe I’d feel less guilty about it, but the pattern would probably still be there, because it doesn’t feel like it’s just about weight. It feels like I can’t get into a normal rhythm with food."
